But then it kept lining up, over and over, with the neighbors and the office, and I started realizing it wasn’t just a bad week or something.\n\nSo yeah… these thoughts about the apartment, people watching and passing things along, that’s been going on for about a year and a month now.\n\nDoctor: When the depression first started, was it before the problems at your apartment began?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to separate it cleanly, like, “this started first and then that started,” because in my head they kind of feed into each other. But what I notice is when I’m really down — like in that hollow place I was talking about, where I’m barely eating, not really picking up the phone, just sitting there with the curtains closed and not wanting to deal with anybody — that’s when I’m also the most sure about the apartment people.\n\nLike, those are the times I feel it the strongest, that they’re tracking when I come and go, talking to the office, keeping notes or whatever they’re doing. When I’m lower, it all feels more obvious to me, like I can see the pattern clearer, even if other people tell me I’m reading too much into it.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can say for sure the depression came before the apartment problems. I just know when the depression gets bad, that’s when the apartment stuff feels the most real and the most intense.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when the apartment concerns were present even when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, there have been times like that. That’s part of what makes it hard for me when people say it’s only because I’m depressed, because there was a stretch — not a long, perfect stretch, but several weeks — where I was doing a little better. I was eating more normal, or at least better than I had been, and I wasn’t waking up with that same heavy hopeless feeling right away every morning.\n\nBut even then, I still felt like the neighbors were watching my door. I’d still notice when people were out in the hallway, or when things seemed to happen right after I left or came back, and I still believed they were reporting on me to the office. It wasn’t like feeling a little better made that go away. Maybe I wasn’t as crushed down inside during that time, but I still thought the apartment stuff was happening.\n\nDoctor: When you’re feeling depressed, do you ever believe that you’re to blame for bad things happening, even when other people say that isn’t true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly like I’m to blame for bad things happening. It’s more like… when I’m depressed, I get even more stuck on what’s going on at my apartment. That’s really why I came in, because I know the people there are watching when I leave and when I come back, like they’re keeping track of my comings and goings.\n\nAnd then there are these notes — people keep telling me, “No, those are just notices on the doors,” like regular apartment notices, but it doesn’t feel like that to me. It feels like they’re slipping notes about me to the office, like they’re passing along information. Everybody says I’m making too much of it, but I can’t just ignore it when it keeps happening.\n\nSo… I don’t know about being the cause of bad things, exactly. It’s more that I feel targeted, like they’re watching me and reporting on me, even when other people keep saying it isn’t true.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were sure the people at your apartment were watching or reporting on you, but you were not feeling depressed during that time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was. I mean, I can’t give you exact dates like on a calendar, because I don’t keep track that neatly, but there was a spell that went on for several weeks where I wasn’t as depressed as I had been.\n\nLike, I was eating a little better — not perfect or anything, but more regular — and I wasn’t waking up every morning with that same hopeless feeling sitting on my chest right away. I could get up a little easier, do a few things, maybe answer the phone once in a while.\n\nBut the apartment thing didn’t stop during that time. I still felt sure the neighbors were watching my door, watching when I left and came back, and I still believed they were reporting on me to the office. It wasn’t like my mood got a little lighter and then I suddenly thought, “Oh, that’s nothing.” I still thought it was happening. So yes, I’d say it lasted at least a couple weeks, probably several weeks, even when I wasn’t feeling that depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that outside forces were controlling your actions or movements?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like someone moving my arms or making my legs walk, if that’s what you mean. I haven’t really felt like a remote control kind of thing.\n\nIt’s more… the reason I came in is because I know the people at my apartment are watching when I leave and when I come back. Like they’re keeping track of me, my comings and goings. And then those notes on the doors — everyone keeps saying, “Those are just regular notices,” but it doesn’t feel regular to me. It feels like they’re slipping notes about me to the office, like reporting on what I’m doing.\n\nSo not controlling my movements exactly, no. More like watching them and keeping records of them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: During those depressed periods, did you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I did. That’s actually one of the things that made me feel like something was really wrong, because I used to have things I looked forward to, even if they were small.\n\nLike choir practice — I used to go, and even if I wasn’t the best singer in the room, I liked being there with people, knowing the songs, having somewhere to be. And bridge, too. I used to enjoy sitting down for a game, keeping my mind busy, talking a little. Sundays I’d make beans, real slow, the way I like them, and it felt like a routine, something steady. And I used to do little repair jobs for folks, you know, fixing this or tightening that, small things, but it made me feel useful.\n\nBut when I’m depressed, I don’t feel that pull toward any of it. The phone rings and I mostly just let it ring. I’ll see who it is and think, “I should answer,” but then I just don’t. Or I tell myself there’s no point going, no point cooking, no point showing up, because I won’t enjoy it anyway and I’ll just be sitting there pretending. So, yes… I lose interest in things I normally care about.\n\nDoctor: Have people ever told you that your speech or thoughts were hard to follow or seemed mixed up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people have said that to me. Not everybody, but enough that I notice it.\n\nIt’s usually when I’m trying to explain something that, in my head, all connects. Like I’ll start talking about the rent, because that’s where the problem began in my mind, and then I’ll go to the marina because that’s part of how I understood where things were being passed around, and then somehow I’m talking about the choir robes or the wires in the walls, and people just look at me like I skipped half the story.\n\nI don’t feel like I’m skipping it when I’m saying it. It feels like I’m following the line from one thing to the next, but other people will stop me and say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” or “Start over.” Sometimes they get impatient, like I’m rambling or mixing things up on purpose, but I’m not. I’m trying to get it out the right way. It’s just hard to explain all the pieces without going into the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you were not depressed at all, but people still said your thoughts or speech were hard to follow or mixed up?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. There was a stretch, not forever, but several weeks where I wasn’t down in that heavy way. I was eating a little better, and I wasn’t waking up with that same hopeless feeling right away, like the day was already ruined before I even got out of bed.\n\nBut even then, people were still telling me I wasn’t making sense sometimes. Because I’d still be trying to explain about the neighbors watching my door — how they seemed to notice when I left and came back, and how it felt like they were reporting things about me. And I know people wanted me to just drop it because I wasn’t as depressed then, like they thought that should make the whole thing go away, but it didn’t.\n\nSo yes, there was a time when my mood was a bit better, but I still had those thoughts about being watched and reported on, and when I tried to explain it, people still acted like I was mixing things up or not making it clear.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like that, no.\n\nI mean, my sleep has been bad at different times, but it’s usually not that kind where I’m up all night and still feeling great or charged up. If I don’t sleep, I mostly feel worn down, foggy, kind of useless the next day. I might lie there awake, turning things over in my head, but I wouldn’t say I’m full of energy from it.\n\nThere might’ve been nights where I slept less because I couldn’t settle, but needing less sleep and feeling fine — or better than fine — that doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself talking much less than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as somebody who talks less, exactly. Sometimes I’m quiet, but I’ve always had times where I don’t say much, especially if I don’t know what someone wants from me or if I feel like they’re just waiting for me to say something wrong.\n\nOther times, once I start explaining, I can go on for a while, maybe too much. So it’s hard for me to answer that straight. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped talking or barely talk now. It depends who I’m around and what’s going on. If I’m by myself, obviously I’m not talking much, but with people… I can still talk. Sometimes people probably wish I’d talk less, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that your face shows much less expression than it used to, even when you do have feelings inside?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really watch my own face that way, you know? I can tell you what I feel inside, but I don’t always know what it looks like from the outside.\n\nSometimes people have said I look tired or serious, even when I’m not trying to look that way. But I don’t know if that means my face is showing less expression than it used to, or if I just have one of those faces when I’m thinking. I don’t smile a whole lot unless something actually strikes me as funny, but I don’t know if that’s new.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed it clearly myself. You’d probably have to ask someone who sees me a lot. I know I still feel things inside, but whether my face shows it less, I can’t really say.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself speaking much less than you used to, even during times when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can separate it out that cleanly. Like, when I’m not feeling low, have I still talked less than I used to? I’m not sure. I don’t really keep track of how much I talk, and it changes depending on who I’m with.\n\nIf I’m around people I’m comfortable with, I can still talk, sometimes a lot. If I’m around people where I feel watched or like I have to explain myself just right, then I might not say much, but that feels more like the situation than some general change in me.\n\nI don’t think I’ve had a clear period where I thought, “I barely talk anymore,” especially not when my mood was okay. Other people might have noticed something, maybe, but from my side I haven’t really noticed a big drop like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know… that’s kind of hard for me to sort out. I mean, everybody has days where they don’t feel like doing things, right? So I’m not sure where the line is between just not wanting to deal with something and not having motivation in that bigger sense.

It might take me a while, and I might sit there thinking about it longer than I should, but I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed that I just can’t get myself moving because the motivation isn’t there.

Sometimes I put things off, but I’ve done that for a long time, so I don’t know if that counts as something new or not. Basic stuff… I guess I manage it, mostly. I haven’t really thought of it as a separate problem outside of when I’m feeling low.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because things at my apartment have gotten… I don’t know, it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m making a big deal out of it, but I know people there are watching when I come and go. Like, they notice what time I leave, what time I come back, who I talk to, that kind of thing. And then there’s these notes, or papers, on the doors and around the office, and everybody keeps saying, “Oh, those are just normal notices,” like building notices or whatever, but it doesn’t feel normal to me.\n\nIt feels like some of them are about me, or that people are slipping notes to the office about me. It’s more that I notice things and I wonder what people are saying, but as far as hearing a voice when no one else is around… no, not really that I can say.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood was unusually high or unusually irritable?\n\nPatient: Unusually high… no, I don’t think I’d call it that. I’m not really the type to get all energized or excited like that, if that’s what you mean. Irritable, maybe sometimes, but I mean, who wouldn’t be when things feel off around you or people are acting strange? I can get short with people, or I’ll feel on edge, but I don’t know if that’s “unusual” exactly.\n\nI guess I’m not sure where the line is. I don’t have times where I feel on top of the world or like I don’t need to slow down. Mostly it’s more like I’m bothered, tense, worn out. If I seem irritable, it’s usually because something’s been building up and I’m trying to figure out what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. Most days it’s there, especially in the morning. I wake up and before I even really do anything, before I even get my feet on the floor, there’s this heavy kind of hollow feeling. Like the day already went bad somehow, even though it just started.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it’s not always like I’m crying or anything dramatic. It’s more like this weight sitting in me, and I’m already tired of the day before I’ve even started it. Some mornings I just lie there for a bit, thinking, “Here we go again,” and it feels like I’m dragging myself into the day instead of just getting up like a normal person. So, yeah… depressed most of the day, a lot of days, that sounds about right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had the feeling that your thoughts are being put into your mind by someone else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it like that, like somebody is putting thoughts inside my head. That sounds… I don’t know, like a very specific thing. What I do know is I came in because something is going on at my apartment, and everybody keeps trying to make it sound like it’s nothing.\n\nThe people there are watching when I leave and when I come back. I can tell because it’s too lined up, you know? Like I’ll go out, and then there’ll be notes on doors, or something slipped around, and they say, “Oh, those are just notices from the office,” like it’s normal building stuff. But it’s not just that. It feels like they’re keeping track of my comings and goings and then passing notes about me to the office, and then when I say it, people act like I’m making too much of it.\n\nSo… thoughts being put in my mind, no, I wouldn’t describe it that way. But I do feel like other people are involved in what’s happening around me, and they know more about me than they should.\n\nDoctor: How long have these experiences at your apartment been going on?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about a year, a little more than a year. Maybe around a year and a month, if I’m trying to place it right.\n\nIt didn’t all come on at once like one big event. At first it was just little things I noticed, and I thought maybe I was just being careful or maybe the building was busy. But then it kept lining up, over and over, with the neighbors and the office, and I started realizing it wasn’t just a bad week or something.\n\nSo yeah… these thoughts about the apartment, people watching and passing things along, that’s been going on for about a year and a month now.\n\nDoctor: When the depression first started, was it before the problems at your apartment began?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to separate it cleanly, like, “this started first and then that started,” because in my head they kind of feed into each other. But what I notice is when I’m really down — like in that hollow place I was talking about, where I’m barely eating, not really picking up the phone, just sitting there with the curtains closed and not wanting to deal with anybody — that’s when I’m also the most sure about the apartment people.\n\nLike, those are the times I feel it the strongest, that they’re tracking when I come and go, talking to the office, keeping notes or whatever they’re doing. When I’m lower, it all feels more obvious to me, like I can see the pattern clearer, even if other people tell me I’m reading too much into it.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can say for sure the depression came before the apartment problems. I just know when the depression gets bad, that’s when the apartment stuff feels the most real and the most intense.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when the apartment concerns were present even when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, there have been times like that. That’s part of what makes it hard for me when people say it’s only because I’m depressed, because there was a stretch — not a long, perfect stretch, but several weeks — where I was doing a little better. I was eating more normal, or at least better than I had been, and I wasn’t waking up with that same heavy hopeless feeling right away every morning.\n\nBut even then, I still felt like the neighbors were watching my door. I’d still notice when people were out in the hallway, or when things seemed to happen right after I left or came back, and I still believed they were reporting on me to the office. It wasn’t like feeling a little better made that go away. Maybe I wasn’t as crushed down inside during that time, but I still thought the apartment stuff was happening.\n\nDoctor: When you’re feeling depressed, do you ever believe that you’re to blame for bad things happening, even when other people say that isn’t true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly like I’m to blame for bad things happening. It’s more like… when I’m depressed, I get even more stuck on what’s going on at my apartment. That’s really why I came in, because I know the people there are watching when I leave and when I come back, like they’re keeping track of my comings and goings.\n\nAnd then there are these notes — people keep telling me, “No, those are just notices on the doors,” like regular apartment notices, but it doesn’t feel like that to me. It feels like they’re slipping notes about me to the office, like they’re passing along information. Everybody says I’m making too much of it, but I can’t just ignore it when it keeps happening.\n\nSo… I don’t know about being the cause of bad things, exactly. It’s more that I feel targeted, like they’re watching me and reporting on me, even when other people keep saying it isn’t true.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were sure the people at your apartment were watching or reporting on you, but you were not feeling depressed during that time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was. I mean, I can’t give you exact dates like on a calendar, because I don’t keep track that neatly, but there was a spell that went on for several weeks where I wasn’t as depressed as I had been.\n\nLike, I was eating a little better — not perfect or anything, but more regular — and I wasn’t waking up every morning with that same hopeless feeling sitting on my chest right away. I could get up a little easier, do a few things, maybe answer the phone once in a while.\n\nBut the apartment thing didn’t stop during that time. I still felt sure the neighbors were watching my door, watching when I left and came back, and I still believed they were reporting on me to the office. It wasn’t like my mood got a little lighter and then I suddenly thought, “Oh, that’s nothing.” I still thought it was happening. So yes, I’d say it lasted at least a couple weeks, probably several weeks, even when I wasn’t feeling that depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that outside forces were controlling your actions or movements?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like someone moving my arms or making my legs walk, if that’s what you mean. I haven’t really felt like a remote control kind of thing.\n\nIt’s more… the reason I came in is because I know the people at my apartment are watching when I leave and when I come back. Like they’re keeping track of me, my comings and goings. And then those notes on the doors — everyone keeps saying, “Those are just regular notices,” but it doesn’t feel regular to me. It feels like they’re slipping notes about me to the office, like reporting on what I’m doing.\n\nSo not controlling my movements exactly, no. More like watching them and keeping records of them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: During those depressed periods, did you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I did. That’s actually one of the things that made me feel like something was really wrong, because I used to have things I looked forward to, even if they were small.\n\nLike choir practice — I used to go, and even if I wasn’t the best singer in the room, I liked being there with people, knowing the songs, having somewhere to be. And bridge, too. I used to enjoy sitting down for a game, keeping my mind busy, talking a little. Sundays I’d make beans, real slow, the way I like them, and it felt like a routine, something steady. And I used to do little repair jobs for folks, you know, fixing this or tightening that, small things, but it made me feel useful.\n\nBut when I’m depressed, I don’t feel that pull toward any of it. The phone rings and I mostly just let it ring. I’ll see who it is and think, “I should answer,” but then I just don’t. Or I tell myself there’s no point going, no point cooking, no point showing up, because I won’t enjoy it anyway and I’ll just be sitting there pretending. So, yes… I lose interest in things I normally care about.\n\nDoctor: Have people ever told you that your speech or thoughts were hard to follow or seemed mixed up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people have said that to me. Not everybody, but enough that I notice it.\n\nIt’s usually when I’m trying to explain something that, in my head, all connects. Like I’ll start talking about the rent, because that’s where the problem began in my mind, and then I’ll go to the marina because that’s part of how I understood where things were being passed around, and then somehow I’m talking about the choir robes or the wires in the walls, and people just look at me like I skipped half the story.\n\nI don’t feel like I’m skipping it when I’m saying it. It feels like I’m following the line from one thing to the next, but other people will stop me and say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” or “Start over.” Sometimes they get impatient, like I’m rambling or mixing things up on purpose, but I’m not. I’m trying to get it out the right way. It’s just hard to explain all the pieces without going into the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you were not depressed at all, but people still said your thoughts or speech were hard to follow or mixed up?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. There was a stretch, not forever, but several weeks where I wasn’t down in that heavy way. I was eating a little better, and I wasn’t waking up with that same hopeless feeling right away, like the day was already ruined before I even got out of bed.\n\nBut even then, people were still telling me I wasn’t making sense sometimes. Because I’d still be trying to explain about the neighbors watching my door — how they seemed to notice when I left and came back, and how it felt like they were reporting things about me. And I know people wanted me to just drop it because I wasn’t as depressed then, like they thought that should make the whole thing go away, but it didn’t.\n\nSo yes, there was a time when my mood was a bit better, but I still had those thoughts about being watched and reported on, and when I tried to explain it, people still acted like I was mixing things up or not making it clear.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like that, no.\n\nI mean, my sleep has been bad at different times, but it’s usually not that kind where I’m up all night and still feeling great or charged up. If I don’t sleep, I mostly feel worn down, foggy, kind of useless the next day. I might lie there awake, turning things over in my head, but I wouldn’t say I’m full of energy from it.\n\nThere might’ve been nights where I slept less because I couldn’t settle, but needing less sleep and feeling fine — or better than fine — that doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself talking much less than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as somebody who talks less, exactly. Sometimes I’m quiet, but I’ve always had times where I don’t say much, especially if I don’t know what someone wants from me or if I feel like they’re just waiting for me to say something wrong.\n\nOther times, once I start explaining, I can go on for a while, maybe too much. So it’s hard for me to answer that straight. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped talking or barely talk now. It depends who I’m around and what’s going on. If I’m by myself, obviously I’m not talking much, but with people… I can still talk. Sometimes people probably wish I’d talk less, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that your face shows much less expression than it used to, even when you do have feelings inside?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really watch my own face that way, you know? I can tell you what I feel inside, but I don’t always know what it looks like from the outside.\n\nSometimes people have said I look tired or serious, even when I’m not trying to look that way. But I don’t know if that means my face is showing less expression than it used to, or if I just have one of those faces when I’m thinking. I don’t smile a whole lot unless something actually strikes me as funny, but I don’t know if that’s new.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed it clearly myself. You’d probably have to ask someone who sees me a lot. I know I still feel things inside, but whether my face shows it less, I can’t really say.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself speaking much less than you used to, even during times when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can separate it out that cleanly. Like, when I’m not feeling low, have I still talked less than I used to? I’m not sure. I don’t really keep track of how much I talk, and it changes depending on who I’m with.\n\nIf I’m around people I’m comfortable with, I can still talk, sometimes a lot. If I’m around people where I feel watched or like I have to explain myself just right, then I might not say much, but that feels more like the situation than some general change in me.\n\nI don’t think I’ve had a clear period where I thought, “I barely talk anymore,” especially not when my mood was okay. Other people might have noticed something, maybe, but from my side I haven’t really noticed a big drop like that.\n\nDoctor: Outside of times when you feel depressed, have you found it hard to get yourself to do basic things because your motivation just isn’t there?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s kind of hard for me to sort out. I mean, everybody has days where they don’t feel like doing things, right? So I’m not sure where the line is between just not wanting to deal with something and not having motivation in that bigger sense.\n\nWhen I’m not feeling depressed, I can usually still get myself to do basic things if I really have to. It might take me a while, and I might sit there thinking about it longer than I should, but I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed that I just can’t get myself moving because the motivation isn’t there.\n\nSometimes I put things off, but I’ve done that for a long time, so I don’t know if that counts as something new or not. Basic stuff… I guess I manage it, mostly. I haven’t really thought of it as a separate problem outside of when I’m feeling low.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I might lie there awake, turning things over in my head, but I wouldn’t say I’m full of energy from it.\n\nThere might’ve been nights where I slept less because I couldn’t settle, but needing less sleep and feeling fine — or better than fine — that doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself talking much less than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as somebody who talks less, exactly. Sometimes I’m quiet, but I’ve always had times where I don’t say much, especially if I don’t know what someone wants from me or if I feel like they’re just waiting for me to say something wrong.\n\nOther times, once I start explaining, I can go on for a while, maybe too much. So it’s hard for me to answer that straight. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped talking or barely talk now. It depends who I’m around and what’s going on. If I’m by myself, obviously I’m not talking much, but with people… I can still talk. Sometimes people probably wish I’d talk less, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that your face shows much less expression than it used to, even when you do have feelings inside?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really watch my own face that way, you know? I can tell you what I feel inside, but I don’t always know what it looks like from the outside.\n\nSometimes people have said I look tired or serious, even when I’m not trying to look that way. But I don’t know if that means my face is showing less expression than it used to, or if I just have one of those faces when I’m thinking. I don’t smile a whole lot unless something actually strikes me as funny, but I don’t know if that’s new.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed it clearly myself. You’d probably have to ask someone who sees me a lot. I know I still feel things inside, but whether my face shows it less, I can’t really say.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself speaking much less than you used to, even during times when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can separate it out that cleanly. Like, when I’m not feeling low, have I still talked less than I used to? I’m not sure. I don’t really keep track of how much I talk, and it changes depending on who I’m with.\n\nIf I’m around people I’m comfortable with, I can still talk, sometimes a lot. If I’m around people where I feel watched or like I have to explain myself just right, then I might not say much, but that feels more like the situation than some general change in me.\n\nI don’t think I’ve had a clear period where I thought, “I barely talk anymore,” especially not when my mood was okay. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I wouldn’t say completely free. There was a stretch, maybe several weeks, where I wasn’t down in that heavy way. I was eating a little better, and when I woke up I didn’t have that same hopeless feeling right away, like first thing in the morning. So in that sense it was better.

That didn’t really go away just because my mood had lifted some. It was like one part of me was doing better, but that other worry was still sitting there.

So if you mean was the depression completely gone, I think it was mostly better for that period. But I wasn’t free of everything, because I still felt like I was being watched.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.1, F20.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because things at my apartment have gotten… I don’t know, it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m making a big deal out of it, but I know people there are watching when I come and go. Like, they notice what time I leave, what time I come back, who I talk to, that kind of thing. And then there’s these notes, or papers, on the doors and around the office, and everybody keeps saying, “Oh, those are just normal notices,” like building notices or whatever, but it doesn’t feel normal to me.\n\nIt feels like some of them are about me, or that people are slipping notes to the office about me. Like they’re keeping track. I can tell by the way things show up after I’ve done something, or how people act when I walk by. I know they’ll say it’s nothing, but it’s been bothering me enough that I figured I better come talk to somebody before it gets worse or before they twist it around more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think so in the way people usually mean it. Like, I’m not sitting in my apartment hearing somebody talking clear as day when nobody’s there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I hear noises in the building, people in the hallway, doors, voices through the walls maybe, but it’s hard to tell in apartments because sound carries weird. So I don’t want to say “voices” like it’s coming out of nowhere, because I’m not sure that’s what’s happening. It’s more that I notice things and I wonder what people are saying, but as far as hearing a voice when no one else is around… no, not really that I can say.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood was unusually high or unusually irritable?\n\nPatient: Unusually high… no, I don’t think I’d call it that. I’m not really the type to get all energized or excited like that, if that’s what you mean. Irritable, maybe sometimes, but I mean, who wouldn’t be when things feel off around you or people are acting strange? I can get short with people, or I’ll feel on edge, but I don’t know if that’s “unusual” exactly.\n\nI guess I’m not sure where the line is. I don’t have times where I feel on top of the world or like I don’t need to slow down. Mostly it’s more like I’m bothered, tense, worn out. If I seem irritable, it’s usually because something’s been building up and I’m trying to figure out what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. Most days it’s there, especially in the morning. I wake up and before I even really do anything, before I even get my feet on the floor, there’s this heavy kind of hollow feeling. Like the day already went bad somehow, even though it just started.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it’s not always like I’m crying or anything dramatic. It’s more like this weight sitting in me, and I’m already tired of the day before I’ve even started it. Some mornings I just lie there for a bit, thinking, “Here we go again,” and it feels like I’m dragging myself into the day instead of just getting up like a normal person. So, yeah… depressed most of the day, a lot of days, that sounds about right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had the feeling that your thoughts are being put into your mind by someone else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it like that, like somebody is putting thoughts inside my head. That sounds… I don’t know, like a very specific thing. What I do know is I came in because something is going on at my apartment, and everybody keeps trying to make it sound like it’s nothing.\n\nThe people there are watching when I leave and when I come back. I can tell because it’s too lined up, you know? Like I’ll go out, and then there’ll be notes on doors, or something slipped around, and they say, “Oh, those are just notices from the office,” like it’s normal building stuff. But it’s not just that. It feels like they’re keeping track of my comings and goings and then passing notes about me to the office, and then when I say it, people act like I’m making too much of it.\n\nSo… thoughts being put in my mind, no, I wouldn’t describe it that way. But I do feel like other people are involved in what’s happening around me, and they know more about me than they should.\n\nDoctor: How long have these experiences at your apartment been going on?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about a year, a little more than a year. Maybe around a year and a month, if I’m trying to place it right.\n\nIt didn’t all come on at once like one big event. At first it was just little things I noticed, and I thought maybe I was just being careful or maybe the building was busy. But then it kept lining up, over and over, with the neighbors and the office, and I started realizing it wasn’t just a bad week or something.\n\nSo yeah… these thoughts about the apartment, people watching and passing things along, that’s been going on for about a year and a month now.\n\nDoctor: When the depression first started, was it before the problems at your apartment began?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to separate it cleanly, like, “this started first and then that started,” because in my head they kind of feed into each other. But what I notice is when I’m really down — like in that hollow place I was talking about, where I’m barely eating, not really picking up the phone, just sitting there with the curtains closed and not wanting to deal with anybody — that’s when I’m also the most sure about the apartment people.\n\nLike, those are the times I feel it the strongest, that they’re tracking when I come and go, talking to the office, keeping notes or whatever they’re doing. When I’m lower, it all feels more obvious to me, like I can see the pattern clearer, even if other people tell me I’m reading too much into it.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can say for sure the depression came before the apartment problems. I just know when the depression gets bad, that’s when the apartment stuff feels the most real and the most intense.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when the apartment concerns were present even when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, there have been times like that. That’s part of what makes it hard for me when people say it’s only because I’m depressed, because there was a stretch — not a long, perfect stretch, but several weeks — where I was doing a little better. I was eating more normal, or at least better than I had been, and I wasn’t waking up with that same heavy hopeless feeling right away every morning.\n\nBut even then, I still felt like the neighbors were watching my door. I’d still notice when people were out in the hallway, or when things seemed to happen right after I left or came back, and I still believed they were reporting on me to the office. It wasn’t like feeling a little better made that go away. Maybe I wasn’t as crushed down inside during that time, but I still thought the apartment stuff was happening.\n\nDoctor: When you’re feeling depressed, do you ever believe that you’re to blame for bad things happening, even when other people say that isn’t true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly like I’m to blame for bad things happening. It’s more like… when I’m depressed, I get even more stuck on what’s going on at my apartment. That’s really why I came in, because I know the people there are watching when I leave and when I come back, like they’re keeping track of my comings and goings.\n\nAnd then there are these notes — people keep telling me, “No, those are just notices on the doors,” like regular apartment notices, but it doesn’t feel like that to me. It feels like they’re slipping notes about me to the office, like they’re passing along information. Everybody says I’m making too much of it, but I can’t just ignore it when it keeps happening.\n\nSo… I don’t know about being the cause of bad things, exactly. It’s more that I feel targeted, like they’re watching me and reporting on me, even when other people keep saying it isn’t true.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were sure the people at your apartment were watching or reporting on you, but you were not feeling depressed during that time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was. I mean, I can’t give you exact dates like on a calendar, because I don’t keep track that neatly, but there was a spell that went on for several weeks where I wasn’t as depressed as I had been.\n\nLike, I was eating a little better — not perfect or anything, but more regular — and I wasn’t waking up every morning with that same hopeless feeling sitting on my chest right away. I could get up a little easier, do a few things, maybe answer the phone once in a while.\n\nBut the apartment thing didn’t stop during that time. I still felt sure the neighbors were watching my door, watching when I left and came back, and I still believed they were reporting on me to the office. It wasn’t like my mood got a little lighter and then I suddenly thought, “Oh, that’s nothing.” I still thought it was happening. So yes, I’d say it lasted at least a couple weeks, probably several weeks, even when I wasn’t feeling that depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that outside forces were controlling your actions or movements?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like someone moving my arms or making my legs walk, if that’s what you mean. I haven’t really felt like a remote control kind of thing.\n\nIt’s more… the reason I came in is because I know the people at my apartment are watching when I leave and when I come back. Like they’re keeping track of me, my comings and goings. And then those notes on the doors — everyone keeps saying, “Those are just regular notices,” but it doesn’t feel regular to me. It feels like they’re slipping notes about me to the office, like reporting on what I’m doing.\n\nSo not controlling my movements exactly, no. More like watching them and keeping records of them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: During those depressed periods, did you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I did. That’s actually one of the things that made me feel like something was really wrong, because I used to have things I looked forward to, even if they were small.\n\nLike choir practice — I used to go, and even if I wasn’t the best singer in the room, I liked being there with people, knowing the songs, having somewhere to be. And bridge, too. I used to enjoy sitting down for a game, keeping my mind busy, talking a little. Sundays I’d make beans, real slow, the way I like them, and it felt like a routine, something steady. And I used to do little repair jobs for folks, you know, fixing this or tightening that, small things, but it made me feel useful.\n\nBut when I’m depressed, I don’t feel that pull toward any of it. The phone rings and I mostly just let it ring. I’ll see who it is and think, “I should answer,” but then I just don’t. Or I tell myself there’s no point going, no point cooking, no point showing up, because I won’t enjoy it anyway and I’ll just be sitting there pretending. So, yes… I lose interest in things I normally care about.\n\nDoctor: Have people ever told you that your speech or thoughts were hard to follow or seemed mixed up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people have said that to me. Not everybody, but enough that I notice it.\n\nIt’s usually when I’m trying to explain something that, in my head, all connects. Like I’ll start talking about the rent, because that’s where the problem began in my mind, and then I’ll go to the marina because that’s part of how I understood where things were being passed around, and then somehow I’m talking about the choir robes or the wires in the walls, and people just look at me like I skipped half the story.\n\nI don’t feel like I’m skipping it when I’m saying it. It feels like I’m following the line from one thing to the next, but other people will stop me and say, “Wait, what are you talking about?” or “Start over.” Sometimes they get impatient, like I’m rambling or mixing things up on purpose, but I’m not. I’m trying to get it out the right way. It’s just hard to explain all the pieces without going into the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you were not depressed at all, but people still said your thoughts or speech were hard to follow or mixed up?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. There was a stretch, not forever, but several weeks where I wasn’t down in that heavy way. I was eating a little better, and I wasn’t waking up with that same hopeless feeling right away, like the day was already ruined before I even got out of bed.\n\nBut even then, people were still telling me I wasn’t making sense sometimes. Because I’d still be trying to explain about the neighbors watching my door — how they seemed to notice when I left and came back, and how it felt like they were reporting things about me. And I know people wanted me to just drop it because I wasn’t as depressed then, like they thought that should make the whole thing go away, but it didn’t.\n\nSo yes, there was a time when my mood was a bit better, but I still had those thoughts about being watched and reported on, and when I tried to explain it, people still acted like I was mixing things up or not making it clear.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like that, no.\n\nI mean, my sleep has been bad at different times, but it’s usually not that kind where I’m up all night and still feeling great or charged up. If I don’t sleep, I mostly feel worn down, foggy, kind of useless the next day. I might lie there awake, turning things over in my head, but I wouldn’t say I’m full of energy from it.\n\nThere might’ve been nights where I slept less because I couldn’t settle, but needing less sleep and feeling fine — or better than fine — that doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself talking much less than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as somebody who talks less, exactly. Sometimes I’m quiet, but I’ve always had times where I don’t say much, especially if I don’t know what someone wants from me or if I feel like they’re just waiting for me to say something wrong.\n\nOther times, once I start explaining, I can go on for a while, maybe too much. So it’s hard for me to answer that straight. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped talking or barely talk now. It depends who I’m around and what’s going on. If I’m by myself, obviously I’m not talking much, but with people… I can still talk. Sometimes people probably wish I’d talk less, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that your face shows much less expression than it used to, even when you do have feelings inside?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really watch my own face that way, you know? I can tell you what I feel inside, but I don’t always know what it looks like from the outside.\n\nSometimes people have said I look tired or serious, even when I’m not trying to look that way. But I don’t know if that means my face is showing less expression than it used to, or if I just have one of those faces when I’m thinking. I don’t smile a whole lot unless something actually strikes me as funny, but I don’t know if that’s new.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed it clearly myself. You’d probably have to ask someone who sees me a lot. I know I still feel things inside, but whether my face shows it less, I can’t really say.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself speaking much less than you used to, even during times when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can separate it out that cleanly. Like, when I’m not feeling low, have I still talked less than I used to? I’m not sure. I don’t really keep track of how much I talk, and it changes depending on who I’m with.\n\nIf I’m around people I’m comfortable with, I can still talk, sometimes a lot. If I’m around people where I feel watched or like I have to explain myself just right, then I might not say much, but that feels more like the situation than some general change in me.\n\nI don’t think I’ve had a clear period where I thought, “I barely talk anymore,” especially not when my mood was okay. Other people might have noticed something, maybe, but from my side I haven’t really noticed a big drop like that.\n\nDoctor: Outside of times when you feel depressed, have you found it hard to get yourself to do basic things because your motivation just isn’t there?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s kind of hard for me to sort out. I mean, everybody has days where they don’t feel like doing things, right? So I’m not sure where the line is between just not wanting to deal with something and not having motivation in that bigger sense.\n\nWhen I’m not feeling depressed, I can usually still get myself to do basic things if I really have to. It might take me a while, and I might sit there thinking about it longer than I should, but I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed that I just can’t get myself moving because the motivation isn’t there.\n\nSometimes I put things off, but I’ve done that for a long time, so I don’t know if that counts as something new or not. Basic stuff… I guess I manage it, mostly. I haven’t really thought of it as a separate problem outside of when I’m feeling low.\n\nDoctor: During the several weeks when you were not feeling depressed, were you completely free of depression the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say completely free.
